13 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
14 
Q.  
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony?   
15 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to demonstrate how the 
16 
Companies’ proposals in these proceedings comport with the requirements 
17 
of the CLCPA.  In the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Public 
18 
Service Commission (“Commission”) found that Sections 7(2) and 7(3) of 
19 
the CLCPA were applicable to rate cases.  Under Section 7(2), the 
20 
Commission is to determine whether any administrative approvals issued 
21 
in these cases would be “inconsistent with or will interfere with the 
22 


--- Page 7 ---
 
Page 5 of 70 
 
attainment of the statewide greenhouse gas emissions limits established” 
1 
in the CLCPA.  Under Section 7(3), the Commission is to determine 
2 
whether administrative approvals issued in these cases would 
3 
“disproportionately burden disadvantaged communities” and shall also 
4 
“prioritize reductions of greenhouse gas emissions and co-pollutants in 
5 
disadvantaged communities . . . .” 
6 
 
7 
 
As we summarize below, the proposals presented by the Companies are 
8 
projected to achieve cumulative greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions 
9 
reductions for KEDNY of at least 886,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide 
10 
equivalent (MT CO2e) and for KEDLI at least 793,000 MT CO2e over the 
11 
proposed four-year rate plan presented in the filings.  By the end of the 
12 
four-year period, and not accounting for growth from customer additions 
13 
or increased demand, KEDNY will have reduced GHG emissions from 
14 
pre-rate case levels by 374,000 MT CO2e and KEDLI by 324,000 MT 
15 
CO2e.   
16 
 
17 
 
The Companies’ filings also include new and continuing initiatives to 
18 
benefit customers in Disadvantaged Communities.  These include 
19 
programs to enhance access to the Companies’ energy efficiency 
20 
programs, thereby saving customers money and reducing emissions.  They 
21 
also include initiatives to increase participation in the Companies’ Energy 
22 


--- Page 8 ---
 
Page 6 of 70 
 
Affordability Program (“EAP”), making customers eligible for other 
1 
benefits and savings.  The Companies’ infrastructure programs also 
2 
include consideration of effects of projects on Disadvantaged 
3 
Communities.   
4 
 
5 
 
In addition to describing how the Companies’ filings are consistent with 
6 
and advance the goals of the CLCPA, the Panel also describes the 
7 
Companies’ four proposed earnings adjustment mechanisms (“EAMs”).   
8 
 
9

Q. 
How is the remainder of your testimony structure? 
10 
A. 
The remainder of the testimony:  
11 
(1) Describes National Grid’s support for New York’s clean energy 
12 
transition. 
13 
(2) Explains how these proceedings fit in the context of the various 
14 
ongoing CLCPA-related proceedings and activities. 
15 
(3) Summarizes how the proposals in these proceedings advance the 
16 
State’s energy transition, achieve results that are consistent with the goals 
17 
of the CLCPA, and satisfy the requirements of the Commission’s orders 
18 
on CLCPA implementation. This includes summarizing the Companies 
19 
assessments of GHG impacts from the proposals in the cases, as well as 
20 
how the rate filings may affect Disadvantaged Communities. 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
 
Page 8 of 70 
 
(4) Finally, the Panel describes four earnings adjustment mechanisms 
1 
(“EAMs”) the Companies propose for the period covered by these 
2 
proceedings.

4 
III. 
National Grid’s Commitment to New York’s Net Zero Future 
5 
Q. 
Please describe National Grid’s commitment to a net zero GHG 
6 
future.   
7 
A. 
National Grid’s vision is to be at the heart of a clean, fair, and affordable 
8 
energy future.  The importance of moving to a low-carbon future cannot 
9 
be overstated.  Economies around the world must reduce their carbon 
10 
emissions and implement sustainable solutions if we are to avoid the worst 
11 
impacts of climate change.  At the same time, the transition must be fair 
12 
and equitable, ensuring all customers, particularly the most vulnerable, 
13 
have the opportunity to benefit from the transition; it should avoid harm to 
14 
Disadvantaged Communities; and it must be affordable so that no 
15 
customers are left behind.    
16 
 
17 
The clean energy transition also must reliably meet customers’ energy 
18 
needs. If the process of transitioning to a decarbonized energy system 
19 
erodes reliability, it will adversely impact the health and economic 
20 
wellbeing of the State and its citizens and reduce the public’s trust in the 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
 
Page 9 of 70 
 
benefits of the transition, which will slow the ability to reach our collective 
1 
decarbonization goals.   
2 
  
3 
Q. 
What is National Grid’s Responsible Business Charter? 
4 
A. 
The Responsible Business Charter (Exhibit __ (CLCPA-1)) describes 
5 
National Grid’s approach and commitment to business operations.  It 
6 
recognizes that businesses in society—and especially businesses such as 
7 
ours that provide essential services to customers and communities—must 
8 
work towards better ways of doing things for the wellbeing of people and 
9 
the planet.  The Charter describes National Grid’s groupwide 
10 
commitments in the areas we believe we can have the most impact on 
11 
society: the environment, the communities we serve, our people, the 
12 
economy, and our governance.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
How does the National Grid Responsible Business Charter inform the 
15 
Companies’ filings in these proceedings? 
16 
A. 
The Responsible Business Charter describes National Grid’s commitment 
17 
to reduce its own GHG emissions (typically referred to as Scope 1 and 2 
18 
emissions) to net zero by 2050.  The Companies propose a number of 
19 
initiatives in these proceedings to advance this commitment, including 
20 
reducing fugitive methane emissions from its gas distribution system, 
21 
converting fossil-fuel powered vehicles to an electric fleet, and improving 
22 


--- Page 12 ---
 
Page 10 of 70 
 
the energy efficiency of its offices and operations centers.  The Charter 
1 
also recognizes that most of the markets in which National Grid operates, 
2 
including New York, have adopted net zero carbon emissions reduction 
3 
targets.  Significant reductions in emissions from our customers’ use of 
4 
natural gas must be achieved if we are to meet these net zero targets.  That 
5 
is why the Companies’ proposals in these proceedings include 
6 
enhancements to our energy efficiency programs, projects to decarbonize 
7 
the gas supply through low-carbon and renewable fuels, and targeted 
8 
electrification.    
9 
 
10 
The Responsible Business Charter also emphasizes that the clean energy 
11 
transition should ensure no one gets left behind.  Protecting vulnerable 
12 
customers is central to that commitment, and the Charter emphasizes the 
13 
importance of helping customers understand and manage their energy use, 
14 
and recognizes the connection between the Companies’ infrastructure and 
15 
other investments and the economic health of the communities we serve.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
What is National Grid’s Clean Energy Vision? 
18 
A. 
The decarbonization of heat is uniquely challenging.  Solutions are needed 
19 
that are cost-effective, minimize customer disruptions, and advance the 
20 
goal of GHG emissions reductions.  In April 2022, National Grid issued 
21 
its Clean Energy Vision (see Exhibit __ (CLCPA-2)), which provides a 
22 


--- Page 13 ---
 
Page 11 of 70 
 
plan for achieving fossil-free heat by 2050.  The Clean Energy Vision rests 
1 
on four pillars: First, accelerating insulation and energy efficiency 
2 
improvements to buildings; Second, supporting cost-effective, targeted 
3 
electrification on our gas network, including piloting networked 
4 
geothermal solutions, to electrify as much as 50 percent of the heating load 
5 
by 2050; Third, in areas where full electrification may not be practical or 
6 
cost-effective, providing customers with the tools to pair electric heat 
7 
pumps with their gas appliances; Fourth, and finally, eliminating fossil 
8 
fuels from our existing gas network no later than 2050 by delivering 
9 
renewable natural gas (“RNG”) and green hydrogen to customers.  This 
10 
portfolio approach is intended to support affordability of achieving net 
11 
zero by moderating the amount of required new electric system 
12 
infrastructure and magnitude of up-front costs to customers, support 
13 
customer choice, and support energy system resilience through the 
14 
complementary use of decarbonized electric and gas systems.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
How does the National Grid’s Clean Energy Vision compare to the 
17 
Scoping Plan issued by the New York State Climate Action Council in 
18 
December 2022?  
19 
A. 
The Climate Action Council’s Scoping Plan recommends sector-specific 
20 
and economy-wide actions to achieve the goals of the CLCPA and examines 
21 
different potential pathways to achieving those goals.  As the Council notes 
22 


--- Page 14 ---
 
Page 12 of 70 
 
in the Scoping Plan, the pathway analyses were “governed by foundational 
1 
principles of ensuring reliability of the energy system as fundamental to 
2 
New Yorkers’ welfare, safety, and prosperity and the cost-effectiveness of 
3 
the approaches to achieving the required emissions limits.”  (Scoping Plan, 
4 
p. 3).  Among the key findings from the Scoping Plan’s integration analyses 
5 
for achieving the CLCPA’s emissions reduction goals are that energy 
6 
efficiency and greater end-use electrification are essential parts of all 
7 
pathways. National Grid’s Clean Energy Vision likewise recognizes the 
8 
need to substantially accelerate and expand energy efficiency in buildings, 
9 
and to promote greater electrification of heating to substantially reduce 
10 
delivered gas volumes by 2050.  The Scoping Plan calls for “a substantial 
11 
reduction in fossil natural gas use and strategic downsizing and 
12 
decarbonization of the gas system.” (Scoping Plan, p. 350). It also 
13 
recognizes the role that renewable fuels may play “to meet customer needs 
14 
for space heating or process use where electrification is not yet feasible or 
15 
to decarbonize the gas system as it transitions.” (Scoping Plan, p. 361).  
16 
These findings are also consistent with the Clean Energy Vision, which 
17 
emphasizes decarbonization of the gas network through renewable fuels, as 
18 
well as targeted electrification to avoid gas network investment where 
19 
feasible and cost-effective.    
20 
 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
 
Page 13 of 70 
 
Importantly the Scoping Plan also recognizes the obligation of gas utilities 
1 
to provide ongoing safe and reliable service to customers, and the need for 
2 
ongoing system investment to meet this obligation.  The Commission 
3 
similarly has stressed that failure to maintain safe and adequate electric and 
4 
gas systems throughout the State during the energy transition would 
5 
undermine the intent of the CLCPA.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What are the Companies doing to encourage electrification of heat?   
8 
A. 
As gas utilities, the Companies have limited ability to directly incentivize 
9 
electrification, other than in certain situations such as implementation of 
10 
non-pipes alternatives (“NPAs”).  As part of the Joint Proposal in the 2019 
11 
KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Companies committed to a process 
12 
by which they would refer prospective gas customers who were potential 
13 
candidates for electrification to Consolidated Edison Company of New 
14 
York, Inc. (“Con Edison”) and PSE&G Long Island and LIPA (“PSEG-
15 
LI/LIPA”) as part of those companies’ heat pump programs.  The process 
16 
has resulted with the Companies referring nearly 800 potential customers 
17 
to their peer utilities in 2022 – well above the targets established in the 
18 
Joint Proposal.  The Companies propose to continue the referral process in 
19 
this rate plan, and to work with Con Edison and PSEG-LI/LIPA to better 
20 
understand the efficacy of these efforts.         
21 
 
22 


--- Page 16 ---
 
Page 14 of 70 
 
The Companies are also working to implement NPAs, which have the 
1 
potential to enable targeted electrification as an alternative to leak-prone 
2 
pipe (“LPP”) replacement.  The Companies will continue to look for 
3 
opportunities to advance NPAs in the Rate Year and Data Years and 
4 
propose to continue current NPA commitments, such as annually submitting 
5 
a request for proposal for NPA solutions and for each company to identify 
6 
five LPP projects that could be replaced with NPAs.   
7 
 
8 
The Companies also are proposing utility thermal energy network pilots 
9 
(one for KEDNY and one for KEDLI) in Case 22-M-0429.  The pilots, 
10 
which are planned for Disadvantaged Communities, would evaluate the 
11 
potential for using geothermal networks to meet customer needs.  Among 
12 
other things, the pilots will evaluate customer adoption, rate designs, and 
13 
cost recovery. 
14 
National Grid also has been monitoring legislative activities aimed at 
15 
advancing the State’s climate goals and implementing recommendations 
16 
from the Scoping Plan.  There currently are legislative proposals that 
17 
would require most new residential construction to be electric-only.  If 
18 
enacted during the period covered by these proceedings, such legislation 
19 
would further accelerate end-use electrification beyond what is reflected 
20 
in the Companies’ proposals.  
21 
 
22 


--- Page 17 ---
 
Page 15 of 70 
 
Q. 
What other steps has National Grid undertaken to date to support the 
1 
clean energy transition and achievement of CLCPA targets? 
2 
A. 
Specific to reducing overall GHG emissions from their natural gas 
3 
systems, the Companies have been actively implementing and promoting 
4 
several initiatives and programs.   
5 
• Since 2013, the Companies have replaced more than 1,500 miles of 
6 
LPP (514 miles at KEDNY and 1,060 miles at KEDLI), with 
7 
corresponding system methane emissions reductions of 28 percent 
8 
at KEDNY and 32 percent at KEDLI. 
9 
• National Grid’s energy efficiency programs reduced over 1,800,000 
10 
MMBtu of gas that would otherwise have been consumed by New 
11 
York customers in 2021 and in 2022; the American Council for an 
12 
Energy Efficient Economy (“ACEEE”) ranked New York’s energy 
13 
efficiency program 3rd in the U.S.1  
14 
• To address growing customer heating demand, KEDNY and KEDLI 
15 
have developed alternatives to new infrastructure such as building 
16 
weatherization programs, NPAs, and industry-leading gas demand 
17 
response programs to reduce customers’ peak period gas use, among 
18 
other potential solutions.  
19

pagers/New_York.pdf

• The Companies actively evaluate opportunities to advance NPAs as 
1

3

4

5 
other gas utilities, in the Commission’s Gas Planning Proceeding 
6 
(Case 20-G-0131) regarding NPA evaluation criteria, incentive 
7

8 
any requirements and guidance regarding NPAs from that 
9 
proceeding into their capital planning processes.   
10 
• National Grid is working with the New York City Department of 
11

12

13

14 
when operating at full capacity. 
15 
• As discussed in more detail in the Gas Supply Panel testimony, 
16

17 
identified potential renewable natural gas (“RNG”) and hydrogen  
18 
projects throughout North America.2

charging infrastructure across the state, and effectively implement energy

5 
IV. 
Context of These Proceedings 
6 
Q. 
What CLCPA-related proceedings is the Panel aware of that relate to 
7 
the Companies’ rate filings?   
8 
A. 
Since the CLCPA was enacted and became effective, the Commission, the 
9 
New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (“DEC”), 
10 
and other State entities have taken steps to fulfill the various mandates and 
11 
advance the law’s objectives.  For example, the DEC adopted regulations 
12 
effective December 30, 2020, establishing statewide GHG emissions 
13 
limits: a 2030 limit equal to 60 percent of 1990 GHG emissions levels, and 
14 
a 2050 level equal to 15 percent of 1990 GHG emissions levels.  The DEC 
15 
is required to issue additional regulations by January 2024 to ensure 
16 
compliance with the statewide GHG emissions limits. 
17 
 
18 
The CLCPA also created the 22-member Climate Action Council charged 
19 
with developing a scoping plan to “identify and make recommendations 
20 
on regulatory measures and other State actions that will ensure the 
21 
attainment of the [2030 and 2050] statewide greenhouse gas emissions 
22 


--- Page 21 ---
 
Page 19 of 70 
 
limits established” in the CLCPA.  The Council approved the Scoping Plan 
1 
on December 19, 2022, which outlines strategies to achieve CLCPA goals 
2 
while prioritizing climate justice and Disadvantaged Communities.  
3 
National Grid continues to actively participate in upcoming processes and 
4 
rulemakings that were recommended by the Scoping Plan, such as the 
5 
statewide cap-and-invest program. The DEC and NYSERDA are working 
6 
to create the program framework for the cap-and-invest program and to 
7 
implement corresponding regulations by the beginning of 2024.   
8 
 
9 
The Commission has also proactively engaged in advancing CLCPA 
10 
initiatives affecting electric and gas utilities in the State.  On May 12, 2022, 
11 
the Commission initiated the CLCPA Implementation Proceeding (Case 
12 
22-M-0149) to assess implementation of and compliance with the CLCPA 
13 
by utilities.  The Commission’s May 12, 2022, Order in the CLCPA 
14 
Implementation Proceeding requires gas utilities to: 
15 
• Develop a proposal for a GHG emissions inventory report that 
16 
includes an inventory of total gas system-wide emissions, which 
17 
the utilities filed December 1, 2022. 
18 
• Develop a proposal for a GHG emissions reduction pathway 
19 
study, which the utilities filed March 31, 2023.  
20 


--- Page 22 ---
 
Page 20 of 70 
 
• Include in future rate case filings, descriptions of the investments, 
1 
capital programs, and initiatives necessary to achieve the 
2 
objectives in the GHG emissions reduction pathway study 
3 
proposal. 
4 
• Include in future rate case filings an assessment of the GHG 
5 
emissions impacts of each specific investment, capital 
6 
expenditure, program, and initiative included in the rate filings.   
7 
 
8 
In addition to the CLCPA Implementation Proceeding, the Commission 
9 
issued an order on May 12, 2022, in the Gas Planning Proceeding (Case 
10 
20-G-0131).  Under the Gas Planning Proceeding Order, KEDNY and 
11

12 
• File initial long-term gas plans no later than May 31, 2024. 
13

14

15

16 
KEDLI filed such studies November 8, 2022, and supplemented 
17

• File an NPA screening and suitability criteria, as well as an NPA 
19 
incentive mechanism and cost recovery proposal.  KEDNY, 
20 


--- Page 23 ---
 
Page 21 of 70 
 
KEDLI, and the other utilities filed the NPA proposals on August 
1 
10, 2022.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How do the Companies’ rate case filings relate to the ongoing CLCPA 
4 
proceedings? 
5 
A. 
It is the Panel’s understanding that the Commission intends to consider 
6 
and address broad policy issues that may affect all of the State’s gas 
7 
utilities in the context of the ongoing statewide proceedings.  For example, 
8 
the issue of whether gas utilities should be adopting significantly 
9 
accelerated depreciation methodologies for their gas system assets is being 
10 
addressed in the context of the Gas Planning Proceeding.  Although the 
11 
proposals presented in this case relate to many of the same issues being 
12 
considered in the generic proceedings, the Companies have not attempted 
13 
to presuppose the outcome of those proceedings.  Instead, as discussed 
14 
throughout the Companies’ pre-filed testimony, the Companies are 
15 
proposing to advance targeted, “no-regrets” solutions for addressing the 
16 
State’s carbon reduction and other energy policy goals as part of these 
17 
proceedings; deferring the larger policy decisions to the generic 
18 
proceedings.

Page 22 of 70 
 
V. 
Advancing the Goals of the CLCPA 
1 
Q. 
Do the Companies’ rate proposals provide information that would 
2 
permit the Commission to make the findings required by the CLCPA?   
3 
A. 
Yes, they do.  The CLCPA is landmark legislation enacted to lead New 
4 
York State to net zero GHG emissions by 2050.  The law provides for 70 
5 
percent renewable electricity by 2030, 100 percent zero-emission 
6 
electricity by 2040, and reductions in statewide GHG emissions from 1990 
7 
levels of 40 percent by 2030 and 85 percent reduction by 2050, with net 
8 
zero emissions statewide by 2050.  The CLCPA also emphasizes that the 
9 
energy transition must be just and equitable, including a requirement that 
10 
Disadvantaged Communities receive at least 35 percent of the overall 
11 
benefits of spending on clean energy and energy efficiency programs, 
12 
projects, or investments.  As the Panel explained previously, the 
13 
Commission has determined that CLCPA Sections 7(2) and 7(3) apply to 
14 
rate cases.   
15 
 
16 
In the CLCPA Implementation Order (Case 22-M-0149), the Commission 
17 
directed utilities in future rate case filings to include an assessment of the 
18 
GHG emissions impacts of each specific investment, capital expenditure, 
19 
program, and initiative included in such rate filings, and to describe the 
20 
investments, capital programs, and initiatives necessary to achieve the 
21 
objectives in the GHG emissions reduction pathways study. 
22 


--- Page 25 ---
 
Page 23 of 70 
 
The projects, programs, and initiatives proposed in these proceedings 
1 
would promote substantial GHG emissions reductions compared to 
2 
emissions that would occur absent such measures and provide for 
3 
continued safe and reliable service to customers.  Measures proposed in 
4 
these proceedings also would provide substantial benefits to 
5 
Disadvantaged Communities as provided for in the CLCPA and would not 
6 
disproportionately burden such communities.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What information are the Companies presenting to address issues 
9 
raised by the CLCPA and the CLCPA Implementation Order? 
10 
A. 
Regarding GHG emissions impacts, the Companies have identified projects, 
11 
programs, and initiatives included in the cases that are anticipated to have a 
12 
material impact on emissions, and the Companies have estimated the 
13 
associated emissions impacts.  The Panel also briefly describes the 
14 
pathways study the Companies performed pursuant to the requirements 
15 
approved in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases and how the projects 
16 
and programs proposed in this case are consistent with achieving the 
17 
objectives of that study.  Regarding effects on Disadvantaged Communities, 
18 
the Companies identify projects or programs that are located in or otherwise 
19 
would affect customers in those communities and provide an overview of 
20 
the impacts and benefits.  
21

--- Page 26 ---
 
Page 24 of 70 
 
1. 
GHG Emissions Impacts 
1 
Q. 
How does the Panel present the GHG emissions impacts associated with 
2 
proposals in the cases?  
3 
A. 
To the extent a project or initiative proposed in these proceedings is 
4 
expected to have a material impact on GHG emissions, the respective 
5 
workstream sponsoring that measure (e.g., Gas Infrastructure and 
6 
Operations Panels, Gas Safety Panel, or the Customer Panel) describes the 
7 
GHG impact associated with the project or initiative.  Our testimony 
8 
compiles and summarizes the collective GHG emissions impacts identified 
9 
by each of the separate workstreams. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What process or methodology did the Companies use to estimate the 
12 
GHG emissions impacts? 
13 
A. 
Working with subject matter experts from each of the involved 
14 
workstreams, the Panel determined the emissions change expected to result 
15 
from the implementation of a particular measure.  The details of how those 
16 
amounts were estimated are dependent on the nature of the proposed 
17 
program or initiative.  For example, emissions impacts from LPP 
18 
retirements are based on reduced fugitive methane emissions and are 
19 
calculated using accepted emissions factors and the length of pipe replaced; 
20 
whereas emissions impacts from energy efficiency programs are based on 
21 
projected reductions in gas consumed by customers.  To provide a common 
22 


--- Page 27 ---
 
Page 25 of 70 
 
basis for assessment, the GHG emissions impacts are presented in carbon 
1 
dioxide equivalent (“CO2e”) and a 20-year global warming potential 
2 
(“GWP”) using the DEC’s accounting protocols for GHG.  
3 
 
4 
The DEC emission factors were sourced from the November 2022 New 
5 
York State Oil and Gas Emissions Inventory. In the cases where a DEC 
6 
emission factor was not available, accepted emission factors from the 
7 
United States Environmental Protection Agency (“EPA”) were utilized.  For 
8 
example, the EPA Emissions & Generation Resource Integrated Database 
9 
(“eGRID”) emission factors were applied to calculate emissions reductions 
10 
associated with electricity savings. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What are the results of the Companies’ assessment of the estimated 
13 
GHG emissions impacts of the rate cases? 
14 
A. 
In the Rate Year, the proposed projects and programs are estimated to result 
15 
in a reduction of at least 84,000 MT CO2e for KEDNY, and at least 77,000 
16 
MT CO2e for KEDLI as compared to emissions immediately preceding the 
17 
Rate Year, not accounting for growth from customer additions or increased 
18 
demand.  By the end of Data Year 3, KEDNY is projected to have reduced 
19 
emissions compared to pre-rate period levels by at least 374,000 MT CO2e 
20 
and KEDLI by 324,000 MT CO2e, again not accounting for growth from 
21 
customer additions or increased demand.  Over the proposed four-year rate 
22 


--- Page 28 ---
 
Page 26 of 70 
 
period, the Companies project to achieved estimated, cumulative emissions 
1 
reductions of 1.69 million MT CO2e (886,000 MT CO2e at KEDNY and 
2 
793,000 MT CO2e at KEDLI).   
3 
 
4

reductions by major program area by year for KEDNY.

Information on the specific programs, initiatives and emissions reductions 
1 
reflected in the estimates is provided in Exhibits __ and __ (CLCPA-3 and 
2 
CLCPA-4).  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Explain what the Panel means that the Companies’ estimated GHG 
5 
emissions reductions do not account for growth from customer 
6 
additions or increased demand. 
7 
A. 
The projects and programs the Companies are proposing to reduce GHG 
8 
emissions are independent of the amount of overall gas demand the 
9 
Companies serve.  In other words, if the Companies did not implement these 
10 
initiatives, total emissions would be higher by the estimated amounts 
11 
shown.  While the Companies’ efforts to reduce energy use and gas 
12 
emissions will reduce overall gas consumption, the underlying customer 
13 
demand for gas is the primary driver of overall gas use and any related 
14 
emissions.   
15 
 
16 
As the Gas Load Forecasting Panel describes in its testimony, each of the 
17 
Companies is projecting moderate increases in overall gas usage over the 
18 
four years presented in the filings.  The forecasts include estimated impacts 
19 
of approved energy efficiency programs, reflect anticipated impacts of 
20 
recent New York City legislation (i.e., Local Laws 97 and 154), include 
21 
projections of customer counts and use per customer.  How actual gas 
22 


--- Page 30 ---
 
Page 28 of 70 
 
deliveries compare to the forecasts will depend on how actual conditions 
1 
compare to the Companies’ projections.  Actual gas demand will also 
2 
depend highly on weather, and can be influenced by emergent factors, such 
3 
as new laws or policies, and the economy.  The Companies’ forecasts reflect 
4 
the anticipated impact of known policies and programs to reduce demand, 
5 
promote efficiency, and support electrification.  Although the Companies 
6 
are actively pursuing additional policies and initiatives to reduce GHG 
7 
emissions, the Companies must plan based on currently known policies and 
8 
activities to ensure safe and reliable service. 
9 
 
10 
Nevertheless, the emissions reductions initiatives included in these cases 
11 
and that are summarized in Exhibits __ and __ (CLCPA-3 and CLCPA-4) 
12 
are generally independent of, and not expected to be impacted materially 
13 
by, the overall level of gas usage within the typical margins of forecast error.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Do the estimates above reflect all of the GHG emissions reductions the 
16 
Companies anticipate that the projects, programs, and initiatives in 
17 
these proceedings will generate? 
18 
A. 
No.  The emissions reflected in Tables 1 and 2, above, are associated with 
19 
measures whose GHG emissions impacts can be quantified using relatively 
20 
straightforward and accepted methodologies.  An example of this is the 
21 
proactive LPP program, where established emissions factors are based on 
22 


--- Page 31 ---
 
Page 29 of 70 
 
the types of pipe material involved, and changes in emissions are dependent 
1 
on the length of pipe being replaced.  Energy savings from efficiency 
2 
measures also can be readily expressed in avoided GHG emissions using 
3 
emissions factors associated with gas and electric energy.   
4 
 
5 
Other programs and investments the Companies are proposing also will 
6 
directly reduce GHG emissions; however, they are less amenable to 
7 
quantification with any reasonable degree of confidence.  Therefore, 
8 
although such investments and initiatives would deliver direct GHG 
9 
emissions reductions, quantifying those reductions at this time would be 
10 
uncertain.  An example of this type of initiative is the Advanced Leak 
11 
Detection program presented in the Gas Safety Panel’s testimony.  
12 
  
13 
Still other investments and initiatives will have more indirect impacts on 
14 
GHG reductions. That is, they may provide a foundation to enable the 
15 
Companies to deliver GHG emissions reductions in the future. For example, 
16 
the Advanced Communication Infrastructure spending is expected to enable 
17 
the Companies to operate more efficiently and improve the ability to detect 
18 
leaks in the future; however, this investment will not directly reduce GHG 
19 
emissions in the near term.  Similarly, as discussed in the Customer Panel 
20 
testimony, the Companies are proposing new customer initiatives such as 
21 
an enhanced language access program and a weatherization health and 
22 


--- Page 32 ---
 
Page 30 of 70 
 
safety program.  Although spending for these programs will not directly 
1 
reduce GHG emissions right away, they will provide necessary foundations 
2 
for enrolling greater numbers of non-English speakers in the Companies’ 
3 
energy efficiency programs and enable more income-eligible customers to 
4 
participate in home weatherization, which will drive future GHG emissions 
5 
reductions.       
6 
 
7 
Still other investments may be needed to ensure the Companies can continue 
8 
to provide safe and reliable service and are expected to have little or no 
9 
impact on GHG emissions (e.g., Operator Qualification training, as 
10 
described in the testimonies of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panels 
11 
and the Gas Safety Panel). 
12 
 
13 
Finally, some of the projects or programs supported by the Panel’s 
14 
testimony may actually increase emissions slightly on a temporary or 
15 
sustained basis, but are necessary to enable the Companies to continue to 
16 
provide safe and reliable gas service.  Such investments and their associated 
17 
emissions impacts are reflected in Exhibits __ and __ (CLCPA-3 and 
18 
CLCPA-4).   
19 
 
20

8

9

10

11 
programs may directly reduce CO2e emissions by approximately 67,857

metric tons annually for KEDNY and 57,128 metric tons annually for 
13 
KEDLI, such that by the end of Data Year 3, GHG emissions are projected 
14 
to be more that 271,000 MT CO2e less for KEDNY and over 228,000 MT 
15 
CO2e less for KEDLI than if the programs had not been implemented.  To 
16 
the extent the Companies’ energy efficiency programs are revised in 
17

21 
several incremental full-time equivalent (“FTE”) positions to increase and 
22 


--- Page 34 ---

accelerate the reach and adoption of the Companies’ energy efficiency 
1 
programs.

Other program proposals, including expanded outreach to Disadvantaged 
4

5 
building replica pilot, will also tend to reduce GHG emissions—albeit less 
6

9

Infrastructure Investments and Operations  
11 
The KEDNY Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel presents a number of 
12

13 
annually by the end of Data Year 3, while the KEDLI Gas Infrastructure 
14 
and Operations Panel presents projects that will directly reduce annual 
15 
emissions by nearly 92,000 MT CO2e over that same period.  A substantial 
16 
portion of these reductions are from the retirement of LPP.  Since 2013, 
17 
these programs have enabled KEDNY and KEDLI to reduce system 
18 
methane emissions by 28 percent and 32 percent, respectively.  Other 
19 
projects that contribute to these emissions reductions include innovative 
20

21 
upstream emissions and displace fossil natural gas. As described in the 
22 


--- Page 35 ---
 
Page 33 of 70 
 
testimonies of the Companies’ Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panels, 
1 
the RNG projects will reduce local emissions to the atmosphere at the 
2 
locations where the biogas is being captured, and displace fossil gas 
3 
consumption.  However, for purposes of the analyses in these cases, the 
4 
Companies are only reflecting the avoided upstream emissions associated 
5 
with the locally produced RNG and are not claiming the additional 
6 
emissions reductions benefits associated with the typical life-cycle analysis 
7 
of RNG.

11

17

18 
that such initiatives are aligned with achieving CLCPA goals, the 
19 
Companies’ do not provide specific estimates of the amount of GHG

5

6

2. 
Consistency with the Companies’ CLCPA Study  
10 
Q.  
Please describe the GHG emissions reduction pathways study the 
11 
Companies performed to analyze the impacts of the CLCPA on their 
12 
gas business. 
13 
A.   
The Joint Proposal adopted (with modifications) in the 2019 KEDNY and 
14 
KEDLI Rate Cases required the Companies to complete a CLCPA Study 
15 
to “analyze[] the scale, timing, costs, and customer bill impacts of 
16 
achieving significant, quantifiable reductions in carbon emissions from the 
17 
use of gas delivered by the Companies in their service territories” and 
18 
“provide further analysis on the projects and programs needed to achieve 
19 
specific decarbonization goals.” (p. 77).   The Joint Proposal also provided 
20 
that the Study would “identify potential barriers to achieving the targeted 
21 
carbon emissions reductions and recommended solutions” and 
22 


--- Page 39 ---
 
Page 37 of 70 
 
“incorporate and respond to any findings or guidance of the New York 
1 
State Climate Action Council.” (p. 77-78).  The Study analyzed impacts 
2 
and barriers based on three decarbonization pathways: two electrification-
3 
focused scenarios identified in the Climate Action Council’s Integration 
4 
Analysis and a third pathway consistent with National Grid’s Clean 
5 
Energy Vision.  The Companies submitted the study in the dockets for the 
6 
2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases on March 17, 2023.   
7 
 
8 
Q.  
How do the scenarios considered in the CLCPA Study compare to the 
9 
Companies’ gas demand forecasts?   
10 
A. 
The scenarios considered in the CLCPA Study are not forecasts.  Instead, 
11 
they reflect different combinations of decarbonization measures that 
12 
achieve CLCPA targets by 2050, and project their implications for gas 
13 
demand. These include varying degrees of expanded and accelerated 
14 
energy efficiency measures, rapid acceleration of electrification, and 
15 
integration of renewable fuels, and the CLCPA Study evaluates the 
16 
implications of different technology mixes, without consideration as to 
17 
whether the necessary economic, market, behavioral, or policy conditions 
18 
exist to support the outcomes.  All scenarios considered in the CLCPA 
19 
Study project declines in delivered gas volumes beginning between 2025 
20 
and 2030.  The Companies’ forecasts, on the other hand, are intended to 
21 
inform the Companies’ supply and infrastructure requirements to safely 
22 


--- Page 40 ---
 
Page 38 of 70 
 
and reliably serve the demand that can reasonably be expected over the 
1 
period covered by these filings.  Forecasts consider the impacts of 
2 
economic and weather trends on gas demand, while also reflecting the 
3 
impacts of enacted policies and established programs – including ongoing 
4 
efficiency and electrification efforts on gas demand.   The forecasts project 
5 
modest, albeit relatively flat, demand growth for the proposed rate period 
6 
over which the Companies must plan to serve customers safely and 
7 
reliably.    
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Are the Companies participating in another GHG emissions reduction 
10 
pathways analysis process?  
11 
A. 
Yes.  In the CLCPA Implementation Order (Case 22-M-0149), the 
12 
Commission directed the State’s gas utilities to develop a proposal for a 
13 
GHG emissions reduction pathways study (Study Proposal).  Like the 
14 
Companies’ CCLPA Study, the pathways Study Proposal directed by the 
15 
CLCPA Implementation Order is to include an analysis of the “scale, 
16 
timing, costs, risks, uncertainties (translated into sensitivity analyses 
17 
around key cost and availability assumptions), and customer bill impacts 
18 
of achieving significant and quantifiable reductions in GHG emissions 
19 
from the use of gas delivered by the Utilities.”  (CLCPA Implementation 
20 
Order, p. 26).  The Study Proposal also is to include three specific 
21 
components:  
22 


--- Page 41 ---
 
Page 39 of 70 
 
• A coordinated long-term gas sector decarbonization pathway 
1 
analysis through 2050.  
2 
• A coordinated near-term plan to address actions needed to achieve 
3 
statewide decarbonization targets through 2030.  
4 
• Individual, long-term utility decarbonization plans to achieve each 
5 
utility’s share of statewide decarbonization targets through 2050.  
6 
The Companies, together with the State’s other gas utilities, engaged in the 
7 
stakeholder process set forth in the CLCPA Implementation Order and 
8 
filed their Study Proposal on March 31, 2023, in Case 22-M-0149.   
9 
 
10 
 
Because the Study Proposal does not include specific, actionable 
11 
initiatives at this point, and has not yet been considered by the 
12 
Commission, the Companies’ proposals in these proceedings are informed 
13 
by the CLCPA Study filed on March 17, 2023.   
14 
 
15 
Q.  
What are the main findings of the Companies’ CLCPA Study? 
16 
A.   
Key findings from the CLCPA Study include: 
17 
• Many essential next steps to enable decarbonization are common across 
18 
all decarbonization pathways.  These include increasing funding to 
19 
support energy efficiency and customer-side technologies, policies to 
20 
support development of supply and integration of renewable fuels, and 
21 


--- Page 42 ---
 
Page 40 of 70 
 
ensuring improved coordination and planning across gas and electric 
1 
networks. 
2 
• The extent of customer-side and energy system investments needed to 
3 
achieve building decarbonization means affordability and equity 
4 
considerations must be prioritized and addressed.  Policy and regulatory 
5 
decisions should provide particular support for low- and moderate-
6 
income (“LMI”) customers and Disadvantaged Communities, and 
7 
minimize adverse cost impacts.   
8 
• 
Achieving net zero will be costly under any scenario, but the strategies 
9 
identified in National Grid’s Clean Energy Vision support a more 
10 
affordable pathway to net zero through lower energy system and total 
11 
customer costs compared to alternatives that electrify the vast majority 
12 
of heating demand.  Mitigation of electric peak demand growth through 
13 
the utilization of hybrid heating systems is a major source of energy 
14 
system cost savings.  
15 
• In all scenarios, gas customer counts and delivery volumes are projected 
16 
to decline, though to a lesser degree under the Clean Energy Vision.  
17 
This decline raises challenges to long-term gas network affordability 
18 
that must be addressed through regulatory and policy reforms. Reforms 
19 
to depreciation will be necessary, potentially in combination with 
20 
additional approaches to support recovery of gas network costs, while 
21 
protecting Disadvantaged Communities and low-income customers.  
22 


--- Page 43 ---
 
Page 41 of 70 
 
Q.  
Did the CLCPA Study recommend specific actions? 
1 
A.     The CLCPA Study identifies a number of next steps for the Companies 
2 
based on the decarbonization pathways analysis.  Given the commonalities 
3 
across decarbonization pathways, these next steps are not dependent on 
4 
selection of a specific pathway, and they address no-regrets focus areas: 
5 
(1) affordability and equity, (2) infrastructure, (3) technology and 
6 
workforce, and (4) demand reduction.  
7 
 
8 
With respect to affordability and equity, recommended next steps include 
9 
programs and initiatives to target incentives to LMI customers and 
10 
Disadvantaged Communities, consideration of opportunities to prioritize 
11 
delivery of infrastructure benefits to Disadvantaged Communities, and 
12 
consideration of modified depreciation approaches to balance near and 
13 
long-term gas network affordability. 
14 
 
15 
With respect to infrastructure, recommended next steps include LPP 
16 
reduction strategies to prioritize near-term safety and emissions benefits, 
17 
while assessing potential options to avoid infrastructure investment (e.g., 
18 
NPAs); support for development of in-state RNG production; planning for 
19 
development of hydrogen blending infrastructure; and development of 
20 
community-scale network geothermal systems to retire LPP or reduce gas 
21 
demand. 
22 


--- Page 44 ---
 
Page 42 of 70 
 
With respect to technology and workforce, recommended next steps 
1 
include advancing network geothermal and hydrogen blending pilots; and 
2 
initiatives to support clean energy workforce development across 
3 
decarbonization technologies. 
4 
 
5 
With respect to demand reduction, recommended next steps include 
6 
expanded energy efficiency and gas demand response programs and 
7 
incentives; supporting customer education pertaining to program offerings 
8 
and technology options; supporting efforts to expand the energy efficiency 
9 
workforce; exploring innovative customer financing options; and 
10 
continued evaluation of NPAs to avoid infrastructure investment and 
11 
address capacity constraints.

3. 
Billed Gas Usage Reductions 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ current commitment to reducing billed 
2 
gas usage. 
3 
A. 
In the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Companies agreed to 
4 
operate their gas networks with the objective of reducing billed gas usage, 
5 
normalized for temperature, in their service territories over the term of the 
6 
rate plans.  For purposes of measuring the goal, the Companies endeavored 
7 
to achieve weather-adjusted reductions of one half of one percent in billed 
8 
gas usage as compared to the forecast levels of non-generator gas use.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing to continue the billed gas usage reduction 
11 
goal? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies believe such a goal is laudable and propose to 
13 
continue it during the proposed four-year rate plan.  Specifically, the 
14 
Companies will seek to achieve a weather-adjusted, one-half of one percent 
15 
reduction in forecast levels of gas usage (excluding non-core and electric 
16 
generation) as included in the direct testimony of the Gas Load Forecasting 
17 
Panel Exhibit ___ (GLF-4A) for KEDNY and Exhibit ___ (GLF-4B) for 
18 
KEDLI. The Companies propose to report on progress relative to these 
19 
goals on an annual basis.  
20 
 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 48 ---
 
Page 46 of 70 
 
Q. 
How will the Companies endeavor to meet these goals? 
1 
A. 
There are a few levers within the Companies’ control that can be used to 
2 
achieve temperature-normalized reductions in non-generator billed gas 
3 
usage, namely energy efficiency, NPAs, and geothermal/district heating 
4 
projects.  More detailed descriptions of those efforts can be found in the 
5 
testimony of the Customer Panel and the Gas Infrastructure and Operations 
6 
Panels, as well as the Companies’ filings in the Energy Efficiency 
7 
Proceeding (Case 18-M-0084), the Gas Planning Proceeding (20-G-0131), 
8 
and the Utility Thermal Energy Networks Proceeding (22-M-0429).    
9 
Electrification of heat is another method to reduce billed gas usage.  
10 
Although the Companies will continue to refer customers requesting new 
11 
and upgraded gas connections to Con Edison’s and PSEG-LI’s heat pump 
12 
programs, participation in those programs is ultimately up to the customer 
13 
and their electric utility provider.  Importantly, other factors outside the 
14 
Companies’ control also can influence billed gas usage, including economic 
15 
growth and development; state and local policy and regulation regarding 
16 
decarbonization, gas connections, building energy codes, and non-
17 
normalized weather factors.  Lastly, the forecast levels in the Gas Load 
18 
Forecasting Panel’s testimony are not precise predictors, but rather the 
19 
Companies’ best estimates of future usage.

VI. 
Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms 
1 
Q.   
Please describe the Companies’ approach to developing EAMs for this 
2 
proceeding. 
3 
A. 
Fundamentally, EAMs are intended to drive performance for the benefit of 
4 
customers and to support achievement of policy objectives.   Well-designed 
5 
incentives can encourage utilities to innovate to provide value to customers 
6 
in many areas, including through the delivery of services and programs at 
7 
costs below historic or projected baselines, the identification of 
8 
opportunities to avoid incremental capital investment, and in driving 
9 
customer participation in programs to levels that exceed expectations or 
10 
historical performance.  As the Companies work to advance CLPCA targets, 
11 
EAMs can provide an opportunity to deliver outcomes at levels that may 
12 
not be sufficiently incented by the policies themselves.  In addition, 
13 
encouraging utilities to identify and drive new efficiencies beyond what is 
14 
mandated will be particularly important given anticipated costs of achieving 
15 
net zero.   
16 
 
17 
In developing the proposed EAMs that follow, the Companies looked to 
18 
build upon existing energy efficiency EAMs and consider additional EAMs 
19 
that provide an opportunity to create system or customer benefits beyond 
20 
what would otherwise be expected. The Companies’ proposed incentives 
21 
are intended to maximize the customer value of current energy efficiency 
22 


--- Page 56 ---
 
Page 54 of 70 
 
budgets, reduce risks to reliability and incremental system costs via demand 
1 
response, and expand the reach and impact of the Companies’ EAP.    
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please summarize the EAMs the Companies are proposing in this 
4 
proceeding and the basis for this proposal. 
5 
A. 
The Companies are proposing to continue two of the current EAMs 
6 
established in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, and to add two 
7 
new EAMs, for a total of four EAMs. The EAMs proposed are: 
8 
 
 
  
(1) 
Energy Efficiency EAM: Gas Share the Savings (existing) 
9 
 
 
   
(2) 
Energy Efficiency EAM: Gas LMI (existing)   
10 
 
 
 
(3) 
System Efficiency EAM: Gas Demand Response (new) 
11 
 
 
 
(4) 
LMI Engagement EAM: EAP Enrollment (new) 
 
12 
The Companies recognize that EAMs related to energy efficiency 
13 
performance of all New York electric and gas utilities may be addressed in 
14 
the NE:NY interim review.  If that happens, the Companies will incorporate 
15 
any changes made in the Interim Review as part of the Companies’ 
16 
Corrections and Updates filing. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ current EAMs. 
19 
A. 
The Companies current rate plan consists of three EAMs for each Company: 
20 
Gas Peak Reduction, Energy Efficiency Share the Savings (“STS”), and 
21 
LMI Customer Energy Efficiency Savings. 
22 


--- Page 57 ---
 
Page 55 of 70 
 
1. 
Energy Efficiency EAM: Gas Share the Savings 
1 
(“STS”) 
2 
Q. 
Please summarize the Share the Savings EAM metric. 
3 
A. 
The Gas STS metric encourages the Companies to exceed non-LMI Gas 
4 
energy efficiency targets set in NE:NY while driving unit cost efficiencies. 
5 
The Companies can achieve incentives under this EAM by reducing total 
6 
gas portfolio expenditures and/or by increasing the overall achievement 
7 
above the approved baseline level of lifetime energy savings. The realized 
8 
difference in the expected and actual unit cost per dekatherm is shared with 
9 
customers by being reallocated within the portfolios, allowing the delivery 
10 
of further savings within the original budgets. The savings are split 70 
11 
percent and 30 percent between customers and the Companies, respectively. 
12 
 
13 
Q.  
Please describe how the Companies will measure this EAM. 
14 
A. 
Gas STS will continue with the same methodology from the 2019 KEDNY 
15 
and KEDLI Rate Cases.  The EAM is designed to simultaneously incent 
16 
both higher levels of energy savings and greater efficiency in the unit cost 
17 
to deliver those savings for non-LMI energy efficiency programs as per the 
18 
incentive formula illustrated in Exhibit__(CLCPA-6). 
19 
 
20 
The Companies propose a sharing percentage of 30 percent based on the 
21 
acquired energy efficiency savings, excluding LMI savings, once the 
22 


--- Page 58 ---
 
Page 56 of 70 
 
Companies have met the energy efficiency threshold savings targets as 
1 
shown in Exhibit ___ (CLCPA-7). This metric will be based on Verified 
2 
Gross Savings. 
3 
 
4 
Q.  
How will the Companies calculate a baseline for this EAM? 
5 
A. 
The Gas STS EAM is formulaic, as shown in Exhibit___(CLCPA-6). The 
6 
Companies must meet their respective annual NE:NY energy efficiency 
7 
savings targets, excluding LMI savings, set forth in the NE:NY Order. 
8 
Additionally, targets will include cumulative over or under achievement of 
9 
all NE:NY prior years savings. and will be distributed across the remaining 
10 
years of the NE:NY period through 2025. If the Commission authorizes 
11 
additional budgets and targets, the EAM calculation, including baseline 
12 
targets shall be updated accordingly. 
13 
 
14 
Q.  
How will the Companies calculate minimum, midpoint and maximum 
15 
targets for this EAM? 
16 
A. 
Minimum, midpoint, and maximum targets are not applicable to this EAM. 
17 
Instead, earnings are determined formulaically once the threshold 
18 
obligation is met, as shown in Exhibit___(CLCPA-6). 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 59 ---
 
Page 57 of 70 
 
2. 
Energy Efficiency EAM: Gas LMI 
1 
Q.  
Please summarize the Gas LMI Energy Efficiency EAM metric. 
2 
A. 
The LMI energy efficiency metric aims to encourage the Companies to 
3 
exceed the LMI energy efficiency targets set in the NE:NY order by finding 
4 
innovative ways to achieve more energy savings with the allocated funding. 
5 
This would also direct energy efficiency savings towards Disadvantaged 
6 
Communities further incentivizing the Companies to identify ways to 
7 
exceed the 35 percent of investment benefits minimum mandated in the 
8 
CLCPA. This EAM is also aligned with CLCPA’s goals of achieving an 85 
9 
percent reduction in GHG emissions by 2050 and reducing 22 million tons 
10 
of carbon through energy efficiency and electrification. 
11 
 
12 
Q.  
Please describe how the Companies will measure this EAM. 
13 
A.  
Consistent with the methodology from the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
14 
Cases, the Companies will measure the LMI energy efficiency EAM 
15 
achievement by calculating the annual LMI energy efficiency savings that 
16 
are acquired through the LMI programs. This metric aims to reward meeting 
17 
or exceeding targets set out by the NE:NY Order. If the Commission 
18 
authorizes additional budgets and targets, the EAM calculation, including 
19 
baseline targets shall be updated accordingly. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 60 ---
 
Page 58 of 70 
 
Q.  
How will the Companies calculate a baseline for this EAM? 
1 
A. 
The LMI EAM metric is based on the sum of annual MMBtu savings targets 
2 
for the Companies’ LMI energy efficiency portfolios over a four-year term. 
3 
Annual targets for the LMI portfolios have been established in the 2020 
4 
NE:NY Order. 
5 
Where: 
6 
LMI Baseline = the sum of the Company’s annual LMI savings targets for 
7 
calendar years 2024-2027 as outlined in the NE:NY Order. As with other 
8 
targets beyond 2025, these are subject to adjustment after the NE:NY 
9 
Interim Review; and  
10 
Acquired LMI MMBtu Savings = the sum of acquired annual verified gross 
11 
energy savings (measured in MMBtu) for calendar years 2024-2027 of the 
12 
Company’s LMI portfolio. 
13 
 
14 
Q.  
How will the Companies calculate a minimum, midpoint and maximum 
15 
for this EAM? 
16 
A. 
Minimum, midpoint, and maximum targets will be set using the following 
17 
methodology, which is consistent with the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
18 
Cases: 
19 
 
LMI Min = LMI Baseline * 105% 
20 
 
LMI Mid = LMI Baseline * 115% 
21 
 
LMI Max = LMI Baseline * 145% 
22 


--- Page 61 ---
 
Page 59 of 70 
 
3. 
System Efficiency EAM: Gas Demand Response (“Gas 
1 
DR”) 
2 
Q. 
Please summarize the new Gas DR EAM metric 
3 
A. 
The Gas DR EAM would measure performance across the Companies’ 
4 
portfolio of gas demand response programs.  Because the programs’ 
5 
primary goal is to provide region-wide gas system relief during peak hours, 
6 
the EAM would measure performance across both Companies (i.e., 
7 
achieving above the minimum target would lead to earnings for both 
8 
KEDNY and KEDLI, but missing it would lead to no incentive for either 
9 
Company). 
10 
 
11 
Q.  
Please describe how the Companies will measure this EAM. 
12 
A. 
The EAM will measure the performance of the Companies’ firm gas 
13 
demand response portfolio, which is currently composed of the Load 
14 
Shedding, Load Shifting, and Bring Your Own Thermostat (“BYOT”) 
15 
programs.  That performance will be assessed in terms of units of dekatherm 
16 
(“dth”)/hour.  The EAM baseline will be determined using a logarithmic 
17 
regression derived from actual performance during the preceding three 
18 
winter seasons.  Achievement will be measured using actual performance 
19 
during events. 
20 
   
21 


--- Page 62 ---
 
Page 60 of 70 
 
Q. 
Why do the Companies propose to measure performance in units of 
1 
dth/hour? 
2 
A. 
The gas demand response programs are designed to reduce peak hour 
3 
demand but measure enrollment in different units (dth/day, dth/hour, and 
4 
number of devices enrolled, for Load Shedding, Load Shifting, and BYOT, 
5 
respectively). Therefore, achievement in the programs needs to be 
6 
converted into one common metric for purposes of measuring the EAM.  
7 
Utilizing dth/hour will (i) enable like-for-like comparisons of events that 
8 
differ in length and (ii) encourage the Companies to focus on providing the 
9 
maximum event reductions needed to maintain reliable service and avoid 
10 
the unintended incentive to call longer-duration events. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Why do the Companies propose using a logarithmic regression to set 
13 
baseline values? 
14 
A. 
The growth rate of customer volume enrolled in the Load Shedding program 
15 
has diminished and will continue to diminish over time, because (i) most of 
16 
the customers who enroll in the Load Shedding program participate in load 
17 
shedding events by switching to backup sources of fuel, and the pool of 
18 
customers who have the ability to switch to those backup sources or have 
19 
discretionary non-heating loads is limited, and (ii) due to prior 
20 
overachievement in enrollments the remaining customers in that pool are, 
21 
on average, smaller in size than customers who have enrolled to date.  By 
22 


--- Page 63 ---
 
Page 61 of 70 
 
contrast, the Companies believe that the potential pool of customers for the 
1 
Load Shifting and BYOT programs remains larger.  However, because the 
2 
per-customer peak hour reductions in those two programs are much smaller 
3 
than in the Load Shedding program, customers enrolled in the Load Shifting 
4 
and BYOT programs tend to be much smaller in size. By program design, 
5 
customers in the Load Shifting and BYOT programs also curtail only a 
6 
portion of their load rather than their full load, resulting in the growth rate 
7 
of the overall portfolio diminishing over time.  Therefore, it is appropriate 
8 
to use a regression model such as a logarithmic regression that accounts for 
9 
that diminishing potential, rather than a linear regression, which would 
10 
result in an unrealistic and unachievable representation of the growth 
11 
potential of the firm gas DR portfolio. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How many years of historical data do the Companies propose to use to 
14 
calculate the baseline, and why? 
15 
A. 
Because the Companies’ gas DR programs were still in a nascent stage in 
16 
winters prior to 2021-22, utilizing those winters would lead to non-
17 
meaningful targets in future years.  Therefore, the Companies propose to 
18 
use the previous three winters’ performance to set the baseline for the Rate 
19 
Year, and to carry that methodology forward into the Data Years.  Thus, the 
20 
baseline for the Rate Year would be set using performance during winters 
21 
2021-22, 2022-23, and 2023-24; the baseline for Data Year 1 would be set 
22 


--- Page 64 ---
 
Page 62 of 70 
 
using performance during winters 2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25; and so 
1 
forth. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What methodology do the Companies propose to use to compute 
4 
achievement for the historical values used to compute the baseline and 
5 
the performance year achievement values? 
6 
A. 
First, the Companies propose to utilize the total aggregate performance 
7 
across the entire gas DR program portfolio for each gas day on which an 
8 
event occurs, regardless of event type.  Events can be one of three types: (1) 
9 
test events, generally called either at the start of the winter season to ensure 
10 
customers are prepared to perform during events later in the season, or mid-
11 
winter if no actual events have occurred; (2) actual events, called on days 
12 
where the forecast average temperature is below the program temperature 
13 
event thresholds; or (3) emergency events, called in response to emergency 
14 
conditions on the system such as constraints on upstream transmission 
15 
pipelines.  Second, the gas day on which the portfolio achieves its highest 
16 
peak hour reductions during a given winter will be used to derive the 
17 
baseline values and actual achievement for each winter.  It may be worth 
18 
noting that, due to program design, events with the highest peak hour 
19 
reductions will likely correlate to the coldest days of the year.  The highest 
20 
peak hour reductions are calculated as: the total dth reductions achieved by 
21 
each program during the respective gas DR event ÷ the total # of event hours 
22 


--- Page 65 ---
 
Page 63 of 70 
 
for each program during that gas day.  See Exhibit __ (CLCPA-8) for an 
1 
illustrative numeric and graphical representation of this methodology. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Why are the Companies proposing to utilize actual performance rather 
4 
than enrollment values? 
5 
A. 
Utilizing actual performance rather than enrollment to determine both the 
6 
historical values used to calculate the EAM baseline and actual achievement 
7 
will encourage the Companies to make efforts to improve customer 
8 
performance during events and align with the goal of the gas demand 
9 
response program portfolio. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
How is “performance” determined during a gas demand response 
12 
event? 
13 
A. 
The methodologies by which performance is determined for the Load 
14 
Shedding and Load Shifting programs during events are detailed in the 
15 
Companies’ tariffs.  Performance for the BYOT program is determined 
16 
using a randomized control trial methodology. 
17 
 
18 
 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 66 ---
 
Page 64 of 70 
 
Q. 
If the Companies were to develop new gas demand response pilots or 
1 
programs, would those be included in achievement on the Gas DR 
2 
EAM? 
3 
A. 
Yes, they would, since doing so will incentivize the Companies to devise 
4 
new and innovative programs and methods to reduce peak demand via gas 
5 
demand response beyond that which they currently achieve or are otherwise 
6 
required to achieve.  As noted in the testimony of the Customer Panel, aside 
7 
from the hybrid heating pilot being proposed outside of this proceeding, the 
8 
Companies have no plans for such pilots or programs at this time.  However, 
9 
if any new programs are proposed that will contribute to achievement on 
10 
this EAM, the Companies will consult with DPS Staff regarding the 
11 
calculation methodology, and, if necessary, submit a filing in this docket 
12 
that explains the proposed methodology. 
13 
  
14 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to set the EAM targets? 
15 
A. 
The minimum, midpoint, and maximum targets will be set at 1 percent, 2 
16 
percent, and 5 percent above the baseline, respectively. 
17 
 
18 
4. 
LMI Engagement EAM: EAP Enrollment 
19 
Q. 
Please summarize the new EAP EAM metric. 
20 
A. 
The EAP EAM aims to encourage increasing participation in the existing 
21 
EAP to provide a bill discount to more eligible low-income customers. 
22 


--- Page 67 ---
 
Page 65 of 70 
 
Enrollment in the EAP serves as a gateway for identifying eligible 
1 
customers for LMI energy efficiency and other programs. This EAM would 
2 
encourage further investment and innovation in identifying, reaching, and 
3 
enrolling customers in EAP.  
4 
 
5 
Q.  
Please describe how the Companies will measure this EAM. 
6 
A. 
The EAP EAM metric would measure the average number of customers 
7 
enrolled in the EAP for the 12 months of the program year, which runs from 
8 
December 1 to November 30. The metric for the Rate Year would measure 
9 
the enrollment for the 2023-2024 EAP program year. The baseline for the 
10 
Rate Year would be set as the average enrollment of the 12 months in 
11 
program year 2022-2023. The metric for Data Year 1 would measure the 
12 
enrollment for the 2024-2025 EAP program year. The baseline for Data 
13 
Year 1 would be set as the average enrollment of the 12 months in program 
14 
year 2023-2024. The metric for Data Year 2 would measure the enrollment 
15 
for the 2025-2026 EAP program year. The baseline for Data Year 2 would 
16 
be set as the average enrollment of the 12 months in program year 2024-
17 
2025. The metric for Data Year 3 would measure the enrollment for the 
18 
2026-2027 EAP program year. The baseline for Data Year 3 would be set 
19 
as the average enrollment of the 12 months in program year 2025-2026. The 
20 
average enrollment for the EAP program year will be based on the average 
21 


--- Page 68 ---
 
Page 66 of 70 
 
of the 12 monthly EAP reports in the designated program year filed as part 
1 
of Case 14-M-0565.  
2 
The minimum, midpoint, and maximum will be set at 4 percent, 6 percent 
3 
and 8 percent over the baseline. The natural variation in program enrollment 
4 
over the period CY 2018 – CY 2022 is 1 to 2 percent in KEDNY and an 
5 
average of 2 percent in KEDLI as shown in the table below. This was used 
6 
to determine the percentage targets for increasing enrollment

Q. 
Please describe how customer needs and benefits were involved in the 
1 
development of the Companies’ EAM proposal. 
2 
A. 
The Companies have built their EAM proposal with careful consideration 
3 
of lessons learned from the existing EAMs, customer needs, and the State’s 
4 
energy and environmental goals.  The Companies’ proposed EAMs are 
5 
designed to encourage the Companies to deliver benefits to customers such 
6 
as dekatherm savings and GHG emissions reductions through the 
7 
implementation of energy solutions that benefit customers above and 
8 
beyond that which the Companies would otherwise be required to achieve. 
9 
Along with the continuation of the STS EAM, the Companies have 
10 
identified new initiatives and EAM metrics intended to deliver customer 
11 
benefits and support the State’s objectives.  The Companies then set targets 
12 
for those metrics at levels that will drive behavior to obtain positive and 
13 
measurable outcomes.  The Companies then assigned incentive levels 
14 
calculated using basis points to each EAM based on the net societal benefits 
15 
that each metric could deliver. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the targets the Companies are proposing for each 
18 
EAM? 
19 
A. 
The targets for each metric are shown in Exhibit___ (CLCPA-7). 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 70 ---
 
Page 68 of 70 
 
Q. 
How do the Companies determine these targets? 
1 
A. 
For STS and LMI energy efficiency EAMs, where the programs are funded 
2 
by customers through base rates, the Companies have set minimum targets 
3 
such that each Company must first exceed the targets mandated by the 
4 
Commission through NE:NY to be eligible for any incentives. For the 
5 
enrollment-based EAP EAM, the Companies determined base figures 
6 
would be established using the previous EAP program year’s enrollment 
7 
and targets would be calculated based on a percentage increase over the 
8 
baseline program year.  For the Gas DR EAM, the Companies derived 
9 
baseline levels from performance during demand response events in the 3 
10 
winter seasons preceding each performance season, and targets are based 
11 
on a percentage increase over baseline. 
12 
 
 
13 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to allocate basis points across EAMs? 
14 
A. 
The Company proposes allocating the basis points for EAMs as shown in 
15 
Exhibit___ (CLCPA-9). 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
When do the Companies propose the EAMs become effective? 
18 
A. 
EAM performance is typically measured and reported on a calendar year 
19 
basis to align with annual energy efficiency program plans and 
20 
implementation. The Companies propose that all EAMs become effective 
21 


--- Page 71 ---
 
Page 69 of 70 
 
January 1, 2024 and continue through the end of last calendar year of the 
1 
rate plans.  
2 
 
3 
Q.  
How do the Companies intend to report EAM results and recover 
4 
incentives? 
5 
A.  
On or before April 15th of each year from 2025 through 2027, the 
6 
Companies will make an annual compliance filing with the Commission. 
7 
The annual filing will include a report on the Companies’ prior calendar 
8 
year performance relative to each EAM target, showing the savings, 
9 
enrollment, and benefits achieved, as well as the calculations for the 
10 
incentives earned. The Companies will also provide an explanation of 
11 
targets not achieved. The yearly reporting approach is consistent with 
12 
current filing timelines. 
13 
 
14 
Q.  
When will the Companies begin recovering EAM incentives it earns? 
15 
A. 
Any incentive that is achieved would be recovered through a surcharge, as 
16 
explained in the testimony of the Rate Design Panel. 
17

Responsibility at National Grid 
2
National Grid Responsible Business Charter 2020
John Pettigrew  
Chief Executive,  
National Grid PLC
At National Grid we’re 
committed to being 
a responsible business 
in everything we do.  
It’s enshrined in our 
purpose – to Bring 
Energy to Life. 
We do this by delivering the electricity and gas that 
our customers and communities rely on. We will always 
do this reliably, safely, and in a fair and affordable way 
for all. At National Grid, we apply responsibility as a 
principle every day, by doing the right thing, finding 
a better way and making it happen. Society expects 
it of us, and we know we can make a positive impact 
in doing so.
We are committed to shaping a sustainable 
economy for all, in partnership with others in 
our industry and beyond.
Society’s expectations
Society’s expectations of business and the role 
business plays in the world are changing. We share 
the belief that business needs to stand for something 
more than profitability. Now, more than ever, we have 
a responsibility to demonstrate our contribution to 
society more broadly – whether that’s by making 
sure young people today become the problem 
solvers of tomorrow, or in tackling climate change 
by reducing our own emissions and helping our 
customers use energy more efficiently.
Our response
Responsibility and transparency have always been 
important to us at National Grid. In 2019, we looked 
at where we can create the most positive impact 
on society. We adapted the way we manage our 
business responsibly, looking at everything from 
our strategic investment process to our role in the 
community and our business processes and policies. 
We applied this framework to shape our COVID-19 
response and showed that it is fit for purpose.
To further our ambition, we are joining the B Team, 
a movement of global business leaders driving 
better ways of doing business for the wellbeing of 
people and the planet. The B Team principles of 
sustainability, equality and accountability will inform 
our actions and we will share our approach 
with others.
We support the United Nations Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs), focusing on the areas 
where we can make a material difference.
Our charter
This charter is our articulation of what ‘responsibility’ 
means for us at National Grid.
We have identified where we can have the most 
impact on society: the environment, the communities 
we serve, our people, the economy, and our 
governance. For each, we have set out commitments 
and how we will achieve them.
We will report on our progress in our new annual 
Responsible Business Report from 2021.*ll 
commitment dates refer to the end of the 
calendar year. We will report on progress on a 
financial year basis.
 * All commitment dates refer to the end of the calendar year. We will report on progress on a financial year basis.

The environment 
We will enable a fair and affordable transition to a clean energy economy, 
and reduce our own emissions.
Our communities 
We will deliver sustainable energy safely, reliably and affordably, 
ensuring no one gets left behind.
Our people 
We will develop the right skills to enable and accelerate the energy transition, 
and strive to build a diverse workforce and inclusive culture.
The economy 
We will power and heat society, and partner with regulators, our business 
partners, suppliers and other key stakeholders.
Our governance 
We will make sure our governance mechanisms reflect our commitments, 
and that the principles of responsibility guide us in everything we do.

Our journey so far
Climate change is the defining challenge of this 
generation. The decisions we take now will influence 
the future of our planet and life on earth. We must 
make significant changes to curb harmful emissions.
Since 1990, we’ve reduced our direct (Scope 1 and 2) 
emissions by 70% (as at March 2020). That’s well in 
excess of our interim target, which was 45% by 2020. 
But there is much more we can do.
 
Most of the markets in which we operate have 
announced net zero carbon reduction targets, 
and we anticipate further legislation. We embrace 
the opportunity to play our role in helping achieve 
these challenging goals.

The environment
What we will do
Our commitments
This charter represents the next stage of our 
environmental commitments, setting out a 
significant change in what we will achieve.
•	 Achieve net zero by 2050. We will reduce 
Scope 1 and 2 greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions 
80% by 2030, 90% by 2040, and to net zero 
by 2050 from a 1990 baseline.2
	 Our Scope 1 and 2 target aligns to a well-below 
two degrees pathway consistent with the ambition 
requirements of the Paris Agreement and Science 
Based Targets initiative (SBTi).
•	 Reduce Scope 3 GHG emissions for the 
electricity and gas we sell to our customers 
(making up 80% of our Scope 3 emissions) 
by 20% by 2030 from a 2016 baseline. 
	 Our Scope 3 target aligns to SBTi's two degrees 
pathway. With SBTi moving to only recognise 
targets aligned to well-below two degrees and 
1.5 degrees, we will continue to work closely 
with the team on this higher level of ambition, 
and work to have the Scope 1, 2 and 3 targets 
officially validated by the SBTi.
•	 Reduce SF6 emissions from our operations 
50% by 2030, from a 2019 baseline.
•	 Move to a 100% electric fleet by 2030 for our 
light-duty vehicles, and pursue the replacement 
of our medium- and heavy-duty vehicles with 
zero carbon alternatives.
•	 Reduce energy consumption in our offices 
20% by 2030, from a 2019 baseline.
•	 Improve the natural environment 
by 10% on the land we own by 2030.
•	 Achieve zero carbon emissions from 
business air travel. From this year (2020) 
onwards, we will reduce our annual air miles 
travelled by at least 50% from a 2019 baseline 
on an enduring basis, and we will offset any 
remaining emissions responsibly.
Our ambitions
•	 Accelerate our net zero target wherever 
possible. We will work to achieve net zero 
in each part of our business as fast as we 
can. Where we need to pursue carbon 
offsetting we will seek offsetting options 
that deliver multiple benefits.
•	 Eliminate all SF6 gas from our assets 
by 2050. Technology and solutions are not yet 
available to achieve this. Therefore, we will work 
with partners from across the sector to identify, 
develop and implement SF6-free solutions 
at the earliest opportunity.
•	 Further reduce our Scope 3 emissions from 
selling gas to our customers beyond 2030 to 
be consistent with the targets set by the markets 
in which we operate (e.g. net zero by 2050).
•	 In the UK, the National Grid ESO (Electricity 
System Operator) will be able to operate 
a zero-carbon system by 2025.
5
National Grid Responsible Business Charter 2020
2  Our interim 2030 target is science-based (80% by 2030 is the equivalent to 50% from a 2016 baseline).
